03 // The Machine That Forgets // I

The Machine That Forgets Memory is not a static archive, but an unstable process of encoding, retrieval, and decay; it shifts, collapses, and rewrites itself constantly. In the last decade or so, “artificial intelligence” has been exposed as a reflection of how (our understanding of) memory operates, and within this operation are its inherent biases, fractures, and capacity for curiosity and invention. Among these technologies, LoRAs (Low-rank Adaptations) stand out as scraps of prosthetic memories, compact neural attachments that learn from, and modify larger AI models. Trained on small datasets, LoRAs become vessels of compressed recollection. They remember selectively, absorbing textures, gestures, pen strokes, and patterns that can later be called upon and reactivated. The selective remembering of LoRAs can be thought of as parallel to the neurological condition of dementia within humans. This disease creates a condition of not only forgetting, but a collapsing of the conceptual frameworks that offer contextual clues as to their proper origin. This experience is heart wrenchingly represented in the audio works of The Caretaker in the album/series Everywhere at the End of Everything. The artists’ depiction of the unraveling of the structural threads that contain memory is presented in a sonic tour through the architectures of human memory in their most frail and failing moments. It is my intention to bind the logics of machine learning systems, human memory loss, and architectural decay through a shared sense of anxiety over uncertainty. Through readings of Hito Steryl’s A Sea of Data: Apophenia and Pattern (Mis-)Recognition this paper will explore how both humans and machines are (not just) capable, but programmed to mistake noise for meaning. Architectural Memory Architecture contains memory. Spaces contain memories, old walls are torn down, types of architectural witness marks are left, with tooling marks and bits of material leftovers. Many of these are hidden below the surface, covered by fresh gypsum and spackle, left to only be discovered by the next entity that tears down these walls; finding hidden messages from years ago only to cover them up again with what they’ve deemed to be the appropriate material, orientation, color, accessory placement or other interventions performed on spaces within their lifespan. We see examples of this even in a city as young as Los Angeles. The easiest example to point to would be the entirety of the SCI-Arc building. Its layers of addition and subtraction repeated countless times has left it with layers of stories. A saturated memory of the palimpsest of information embedded within its walls. Other examples of these layers of operations can be found all over the city of Los Angeles.

04 // The Machine That Forgets // II

The 110 freeway exhibits stairways to nowhere, the odd remnants of the Belmont (Pacific Electric) Tunnel that once housed the Hollywood Subway.The Bradbury Building reveals its temporal layering through cracked marble floors, patched materials, and the uncanny presence of contemporary fire doors rudely inserted into historic interiors. These collisions of old and new produce moments of dissonance, where architectural time collapses into a single frame. Architecture’s role is not merely as a container for memory, but it is also a performative actor. Architecture is not a static thing. It ebbs and flows with time, negotiating nature, destruction, and building codes. Constantly updating itself, gathering new information, morphing into its most recent version, most suitable for whatever its new contemporary context is. Vestiges of previous architectural logic still seep out into the contemporary surface, shifting understandings and contexts as they rise. It is often argued that Los Angeles’ architectural ethos exists in a system of forgetting and erasure, but those pieces of the past are never erased. The materials are shifted to other locations, meant for alternative uses (even if that is just in a landfill) but the material can never be fully erased. Only its physical and temporal context has shifted. Old structures are modified and distorted. In the Art’s District we find the result of its direct connection to the rail lines of the city’s industrial past. Many moments of oddly shaped buildings, wrapped around train tracks with elegant curves, obtuse angles, and extremely long and narrow buildings. Misalignments and forced geometries, blocked in windows that are now structural entities of the building. Memory becomes a spatial constraint, a limitation on the interventions we may deem necessary. These remnants of the past can be seen as an inherited error of the data set. A hole in the boat that must be plugged in order to maintain stability. When a container system breaks down, the result is not absence, but of excess. This failed integrity leads not to emptiness but towards noise, ambiguity, and hallucination. Architecture is guilty of the most apophenic tendencies. Forever imagining. Forever Hallucinating. Constantly working with dirty datasets and incomplete memory. Meaning is not retrieved intact, but inferred. Architects extract meaning where there is none. Design does not emerge from certainty, but from negotiating gaps. Treating degradation as failure leads to two dominant responses. The first is total erasure, demolition, and replacement. The second is rigid preservation, an attempt at perfect recall results in the building being frozen in a simulacrum of completeness, a fabricated condition of wholeness. Both approaches misinterpret memory as something that can be stabilized, or even

05 // The Machine That Forgets // III

contained. In practice, memory always leaks. Machinic Hallucinations As buildings carry with them these scars and memories of past encounters, LoRAs too contain scraps of data, expressed through incoherent memories and incomplete images. Neural networks reflect the training styles and data sets used to create them. LoRAs operate within a more specific context than a full neural network would, utilizing “frozen” weights and parameters to more efficiently perform a set of tasks. In this sense, we can view LoRAs as an adaptive layer to a neural network; a partial memory that modifies larger systems, much like an architectural intervention or restoration would modify an existing wall. This renovation is the LoRAs’ way of fine tuning the original structure without completely demolishing and rebuilding it. Each subsequent dataset becomes an additional layer of memory, adjusting how the system remembers and reproduces visual or linguistic forms. When a LoRA is not trained on enough data, its output is blurry, incoherent, and ghostlike. Images blurred into one another, almost recognizable instances appear, but are not easily remembered due to their uncanniness. The framework is weak, the ability to recollect is reduced to a spectral image. In dementia, a similar collapse occurs. Imagine a bucket of water. This bucket is the container, the water is memories. As dementia progresses, holes begin to form and the bucket begins to leak. The water doesn’t evaporate, but it spills out, churning together, lost in a swirl of nonsense, flashes of light and sound, moments decades apart blending together. Similarly to the way a LoRA loses sight of the context of the image it’s attempting to create, one who suffers from dementia becomes lost in a sea of static, noise and confusion. The disease breaks down the structure and context of the memories, recollection somewhere between the past and now, but holds no solid place in either temporal space. The mind’s vision, much like a poorly trained LoRA’s vision, is a blurred canvas where sensing is useless without the structural context to bind itself to for guidance. A machine doesn’t recognize what a spoon is until it has been trained on hundreds of images of spoons; and even then, it might still mistake a marker for a spoon. This type of incorrectly categorized mishap often happens with dementia patients, their brains no longer recognize the intended use-case of the object, the object is recognizable as something to hold, but its data that contains its function is no longer there. The data within their training set has been scrambled. The meta data has been misplaced, lost from the image, and the tags that

06 // The Machine That Forgets // IV

once guided the user to clarity no longer exist. Noise. Lines. Color patterns. In Hito Steyerl’s writing A Sea of Data, she argues that in a world overflowing with digital noise, apophenia becomes a strategy of survival as well as a mechanism of control . Her brilliant introduction not seeing anything intelligible is the new normal [pp1] reflects a lot of my own perceptions of life since 2015~. Each of our senses completely overwhelmed, our perception of life begins to liken itself to that of a record that has been used so often that it has worn through the grooves until all we are left with is static. Static is not nothing. Audible static, visible static, noise, is all information in one way or another. It can be interpreted and manipulated. Neural networks are designed with apophenic tendencies. They recognize patterns where there are none to be found; they find patterns in randomness, logic in illogical places. They are programmed to find patterns where patterns do not exist. Architectural Apophenia Architects find possibility within piles of rubble just the same as they find pattern within highly-detailed parametric facades that are intended to appear random. If apophenia is the tendency to mistake noise for meaning, architecture is one of its most institutionalized forms. Architects rarely work from complete information; instead, they reinterpret remnants, partial drawings, zoning artifacts, infrastructural scars, historical traces, constructing coherence from what is missing or misaligned. Architectural practice already operates within the same apophenic logic as neural networks trained as incomplete datasets. The difference is not whether patterns are misrecognized, but how those misrecognitions are framed: as error to be erased, or as latent structure to be worked through. Everywhere at the End of Time I came across an album during the pandemic by an artist called The Caretaker which was produced by James Leyland Kirby and they did extensive research and interviews with patients and family members of dementia victims. Dementia is not necessarily a symptom of forgetting but of the containers that hold and label the memories begin to break down. Past encounters, of people, object, and architecture begin to mix together in a soup not defined by temporal or spatial limitations. Objects and people aren’t forgotten, but their context is. As this 6-hour~ collection of tracks plays, it slowly morphs from calming static-y jazz to unrecognizable noise. The notes begin to warble and fall apart. They lose their fidelity until they become an unrecognizable image. By the time the album reaches the final tracks, the sonic landscape becomes otherworldly, somewhere akin to sitting on a

07 // The Machine That Forgets // V

celestial beach listening to the humming of the universe while watching a final sunset. This audible landscape shifts from warm and fuzzy nostalgia into a barren, cold landscape. Notes from the previous tracks sometimes float back up to the surface in a last attempt to be heard one last time before sinking back below the surface of a static and drone sea. This condition has overtaken the original memory. Its temporal location stretched and skewed until it is no longer recognizable for what it used to be. The droning noise, the static is not nothing. There is still information here. An audible sense of movement, of some sort of structure within the frameworks of these drones. The real nothingness is the silence that follows the end of the record. The final track stops and we are made powerfully aware of that silence. The Caretaker’s work operates not merely as a representation of dementia, but as a constructed system of degraded memory. Its progression mirrors the failure modes of both LoRAs and architectural palimpsests. Early clarity gives way to repetition, compression, and distortion. Fragmentations resurface without context and the structure persists longer than meaning. Like a poorly trained LoRA, the album continues to generate output even as its internal framework collapses. LIke architecture under numerous successive interventions, it never returns to silence, only noise to be reconfigured. In this sense, the album serves as a sonic architecture of memory decay, one that reveals how structure can persist even after legibility is lost. Architectural Noise This journey through the stages of memory decay can be likened to that of architectural decomposition. A structure is typically built with a specific function in mind; a tower, an elevated railway, a hospital, a freight depot. However, as these structures begin to age, so do their functions. Architectural advancements call for drilling through facades, routing wires along cornices and molding, mounting cameras on every corner. The building is constantly modified and updated, while the structure remains the same. In many cases, a building’s function requires a drastic overhaul. The spaces are gutted, the shell hardly even remains; yet the memories inside float freely with a loosened context. The graffiti in SCI-Arc that still remains embedded into the walls is an example of this contextual palimpsest. The walls built for a freight depot; once clean and new, were put through years of use and abuse during the height of Los Angeles’ industrial age. For decades this structure stood as a fixture of the industrialization of commodities, a hub for rail lines that stretched out across the United States. After the industrial

08 // The Machine That Forgets // VI

age, the building was eventually abandoned, leaving it free to the community to modify to meet their needs/desires. The context once again is shifted. Pieces of the building are stripped away over time, the tracks that once connected it to the outside world were ripped out, fences were installed, the building lay gutted, its former context removed, yet the memories still remain. Spray paint, scarred concrete columns, fading numbered window bays all point out the architectural palimpsest that has been generated within the building. In the midst of this time of “abandonment” (the building was far from abandoned, it simply was given a function by the community that didn’t quite fit the bounds of what society deemed proper) Laying amidst its own rubble and decay, the building became noise. Long Decline is Over Noise is not Nothing. It is the initiating generative information that can produce images, often where there is no image to start. Steyrel describes a leaked image from Edward Snowden which might express an apophenic condition that could be interpreted as a digital sea, waves over a calm sea, when there is no sea in sight. Once decrypted, this image reveals a picture of clouds in the sky; likely taken from IDF drone footage sometime in 2008. Noise is not nothing. It disguises, masks, distorts and is base material for creation. Just as the molecules of air around us become more obvious during a dust storm; the wind patterns and maneuvers become more obvious to a viewer. Even without the dust, air is not nothing. There is information in static, in drone, in white noise. This information changes the way we interact with the world around us. It influences our lives in ways we so often are not even aware of. The static of an old record might initially be a calming, nostalgic sound, but once that record plays itself out until the grooves are worn down so much that the static overtakes the original recording, the static takes on a new role. There is uncertainty, and anxiety in the amount of noise being generated. While the same information is still present, the context is lost, buried under noise. This same noise that once brought about a sense of calm and nostalgia now brings with it a sense of confusion. The pattern no longer recognizable, we fear what lays beneath its encryption. We look for patterns in this data, but not even the false sense of security that comes from apophenia can save us. We are lost in the collapse of structure as the noise of ambiguity overtakes us.

11 // Node: 1988_Trailer // Narrative

The Demarcation: A short chain link fence, maybe 4 feet high. It never had a sense of authority or security; it was merely a demarcation of a boundary to keep a child from walking into the street.
The Playground: A small yard with wide, scratchy blades of green grass. The lawn terminated roughly around the roots of a large tree where the dirt became the primary site of play.
The Winch: A large blue monster truck, a dead RC car used as a static prop. The coolest part was the manual winch at the front, used to pull toy soldiers out of the dirt by the tree.
The Interior Palette: A saturation of 70s and 80s tones—burnt orange, moss green, and deep brown. The living room was dominated by an orange/green/brown shag carpet.
The Kitchen Artifact: In the north window sat a potato propped up by toothpicks in a glass jar, propped up so the roots could reach down into the water.
The Hallway Logic: Three sequential doors on the right side of a narrow wood-paneled hall. First a coat closet, then the bathroom, then a bedroom containing a playpen.
The Perpendicular Tub: A white porcelain bathtub oriented towards the south end of the bathroom, sitting perpendicular to the long axis of the trailer.

25 // Precedent: Aldo Rossi

aldo_rossi

26 // Theory: Architectural Memory

The Persistent Artifact: Rossi, Gandy, and the Collective Automata In the Rossian view, the city is a theater of human events. Architecture is not merely a functional container but a "primary element"—a permanent artifact that survives the functions for which it was built. These artifacts are the repositories of collective memory; they are the fixed points around which the chaos of the city revolves. But what happens when the artifact is not a marble monument in Milan, but a transient trailer in Green Acres? The Memory Machine operates at the intersection of Joseph Gandy’s forensic ruins and Aldo Rossi’s urban archetypes. While Gandy provided the visual language of the "cutaway" to reveal the hidden layers of structure, Rossi provides the philosophical justification for the project’s obsession with domestic typologies. To Rossi, memory is the thread that binds the individual to the collective. In this zine, the 1992 mobile home and the Clovis rental are treated as "Urban Artifacts" of the Central Valley—singular forms that persist in the mind long after the lease has ended or the wheels have moved. By proceduralizing these sites through Python scripts and Blender's generative nodes, we are not just modeling houses; we are constructing a Collective Automata. We are testing the "persistence" of these spaces. The glitchy, smudged palimpsest of the render is a digital manifestation of Rossi's analogous architecture—a process where memory, place, and history are filtered through an individual's consciousness until they become a new, "true" reality. These images represent an architecture that is stripped of its immediate utility and returned to its status as a monument of the everyday. Like Soane’s Bank viewed as a ruin, these domestic memories are excavated to find the "skeleton" of the self. They are a testament to the fact that the architecture of our past is never truly demolished; it remains as a permanent artifact within the machine of our own remembrance.

Christopher D. Elite 26 Irvine, CA 26 Brunch reviews Mar 14, 2026 2 photos2 check-ins [BUSINESS NAME] is an all time favorite of my family. I have been to both locations, but the downtown LA location holds some special memories. Its high ceilings, beautiful white walls, and fresh flour decorations are luxurious and old world gorgeous. Even the cacophony of voices that echo off the large ceilings add to the luxurious, Italian glow.
My wife and I recently went here for Valentine's Day. I accidentally made a reservation at the other location, but this location was kind enough to accommodate me, which speaks to the service. I will not sometimes the actual food service can be quite slow, but that is inconsistent so I will not damn the restaurant overall for it. However they should improve.
The drinks are awesome. Punchy and alcohol forward. My wife loved the spicy margarita equivalent (forgot the name) from our latest visit. Additionally, there is a healthy wine selection--both local and international.
The food is delicious, rustic, upscale Italian fare. My wife and I have so many favorites: the chicken Parmesan, the portobello mushroom fries, the pappardelle, the bolognese, and of course the tartufo pizza. In addition, the bakery has an amazing selection of desserts, with my wife and I loving the beignets and the macarons.
Prices are high-ish, but not offensively so. Think 30-50 per plate and maybe $75pp with a drink. So not at all terrible. All in, I highly recommend [BUSINESS NAME] and think everybody should try it.
Ambiance: 5/5 Service: 4/5 Drinks: 4.5/5 Food: 5/5 Price: 4/5 Consistency: 4/5

Overall, the restaurant itself is beautiful. The space is elegant, open, and very aesthetically pleasing. Even the bathrooms were stunning -- decorated in gold and white and very pretty.
Our waitress was very nice and attentive, which made the experience even better.
They also have a front dessert counter filled with beautiful pastries and desserts that looked amazing. I didn't try any this time, but I would definitely love to come back and sample a few on my next visit.
I would definitely go back again -- but next time, I'd try a different pizza or maybe explore some of their other menu items (and dessert!).

Aug 31, 2025Previous review Always a pleasure, and never a disappointment after coming here for 12 years. A fabulous French patisserie and bakery, amazing macarons, a woodfired on site, pizza and bread, oven, and need to order Italian pastas, pizzas, salads, and equally great entrées make for a great experience for everyone from small singles and couples to larger parties. Created in the original women's wear department of one of Los Angeles's oldest and most luxurious department stores, this place stands the test of time from maintaining the highest quality and some of the best service available in downtown Los Angeles. Non-validated parking is available across the street on Grand Avenue between 7th and 8th Street. But it's also an easy one block stroll from the 7th and Metro West exit. They do take both Reservations and have a waitlist for tables. The restaurant is also ADA accessible. There are a number of V/V+ selections as well. Also, there's a full bar in the front that features happy hours directly across from the patisserie and bakery. Because this is a large department store space, ambience can be quite noisy and echoes because of all of the white marble surfaces, probably not the best place for intimate and quiet conversations.
Mar 14, 2015Previous review After skating a Pershing Square, we noticed this while walking the revised downtown lofty-artsy-theatry-dog salony-still a few homelessy district.
Since an oddly large number of my friends have also eaten here, and I was just passing through the first-time to check out the bakery, time to eat.
Simple formula - avoid everything Carlyn ate, order things Clara, Megumi and Edwin liked, then improvise per the advice of their unbelievely well-trained staff.
Made the $20 pizza my own: 1/2 artichoke and 1/2 burrata - it's like having Batalli's burrata bar on a perfect 1000F fired crust. There: appropriate value for the price, accomplished (it's about a 16-inch medium-large)
Hit the mezze plates: Grilled octopus - perfect. sauteed kale sprouts - nice. duck prosciutto crostini - can I have a hoagie-sized version of this?
Trenne - triangular penne with a crisp-edge from the pan-fry afterwards with a rib eye-based red sauce that rocks (I'd double the sauce, just because)
Desserts - the peanut butter terrine figured out how to make peanut butter fluffy and wedge it into a napoleon - nice and not overly sweet. Butterscotch budino - went even better with almond gelato added. Ditto with those beignets (which, depending on your comparison with New Orleans, may not strictly really be beignets and more like round raised donuts...) which were great with some more gelato to go with the raspberry puree. Btw, croissants are great here.
The place used to be a designer fashion salon, so the high-ceilings do make it sort of like eating inside a cathedral, din-wise.
As to the difficulty with seating, because the place is so huge and the staff so efficient, we waited all of exactly 16.5 minutes at the peak of a Saturday night dinner hour at 7 pm for a table for 2. Bigger parties, definitely have to plan, because they don't accept reservations. However there is a open bar area to bide your wait-times, and there's always the bakery.
And don't forget to say, "Hi." to the blue-feathered fellow in the cafe area.

Olena A. Los Angeles, CA 8 Brunch reviews Feb 3, 2025 10 photos [BUSINESS NAME] transports you to another world where Italian classics meet an elevated French bistro experience. This location in particular is very grand with the high ceilings and minimalist decor. I would recommend just stopping by to admire the beautiful pastries and macaron display.
I was drawn to the Branzino on the menu which is a tender white fish that's grilled with tender meat and crispy skin. The fish was very well done - so buttery and rich, but perfectly balanced with the light and lemony frisée salad of watercrest. This dish did take a while to cook so make sure to order yourself an appetizer while you wait.
With that being said, I ordered the Portobello fries which are thick cut, portobello mushrooms, in a thick Parmesan batter. There are a lot of great reviews on yelp about these but I did not enjoy them like many others did. I found the powder to be too thick for the mushroom which made the "fry" dense. With the mushroom being a very absorbent vegetable, it was very oily on the inside. With the cilantro aïoli, it was a very dense and oily appetizer - a huge contrast to the light and flavorful fish.
I did, however, find the service to be very professional, and the waiter guided me through my decision to land on the Branzino.
I would definitely come back for a pizza or pasta sometime!

Lissa L. Elite 26 All-Star La Verne, CA 12 Brunch reviews Feb 27, 2024 3 photos [BUSINESS NAME] all-white interiors are the perfect backdrop for the star of the show - the pastries in the display case and the food. Your eye is drawn to the colors displayed on the wall (they offer boxed chocolates and sprinkles-covered pretzels) to entice you into buying their treats. It's a great way to minimize visual clutter.
The service was top-notch. The servers are knowledgeable about the food and the alcoholic beverages offered. They make it a point to ask about allergies and food sensitivities. This is LA, after all. ;)
My short rib was enjoyable. The tender meat along with some polenta and that savory sauce made for the perfect bite.
Their portobello fries were crispy and not overly battered. It was just crunch after satisfying crunch in my mouth. Paired with their green sauce, it was *chef's kiss* an excellent appetizer.
My hubby had the club sandwich and it was an elevated version of the classic. The potato chips were made in-house and didn't feel greasy. Just thick, hearty and starchy goodness!
One issue I had was the lack of any acoustic control. It was difficult to hear everybody else at the table. I'm not sure why, but it sounded louder in there than it actually was. We were there on a weeknight and given that it was around 8pm, there were few guests. Perhaps a sound engineer could come over and see what could be done. The sounds tended to saturate only in some areas.
Another issue I had was that the server kept forgetting my drink order. It was minor, though I had to follow up with him twice, and another time with the guy who brought us dessert. It was only when I was done with my meal that he remembered to finally bring it, along with a slightly audible apology. It's okay. The food and the ambiance made up for it. [BUSINESS NAME] will always live up to your expectations. :o)

Gretel Y. Elite 26 All-Star Gilbert, AZ 105 Dessert reviews Jan 26, 2024 10 photos Restaurant, bakery, and it's pretty? Sign me up.
A friend recommended [BUSINESS NAME] during our short and sweet visit to LA. I asked for coffee and brunch spots and BL checked all the boxes. We didn't have a reservation for the restaurant, and were informed about a long-ish wait when we arrived. However, they said the cafe seating area is first come, first serve and we can still order from the restaurant menu from there. Since we had a long drive back to Phoenix that day and didn't want to wait, we decided to sit in the cafe. It worked out just fine!
We ordered the following: Smoked salmon benedict $32 Lobster hash $35 Le pancake $26 Latte
The smoked salmon benedict came with poached eggs, smoked salmon, red onions, sautéed spinach, hollandaise sauce, crème fraîche, and hash brown potato galettes. The lobster hash came with lobster, two poached eggs, potato, leek & scallion hash cakes, tender greens, and lobster beurre monté. The le pancake was a large soufflé pancake served with fresh strawberries, Chantilly cream, and strawberry compote. The presentation was beautiful. My family of three shared everything so we could sample each item. All the entrees tasted fresh and delicious, but my favorite was the lobster hash. The latte was solid, too.
We had to make a stop at the bakery and take some treats before leaving. I chose a box of six macarons, mostly different flavors as well as their version of Suprême's NY roll, the chocolate pistachio flavor. The pastries were pretty and tasty, but none of them blew my mind.
Our server was fantastic. He was pleasant and responsive. Though the food was great, the portions are meant for a light brunch, in my opinion. Overall, we had a lovely experience and think it's worth visiting!

NAPOLEON The ONLY thing that was good. One of my favorite desserts! The crispy, flaky puff pastry and the pastry cream was done well. Everything was delicious!
CAKE MATCHA YUZU Very sharp, sour citrus flavor that overpowered the matcha. Could barely taste the matcha. Wasn't very good and the cream was overwhipped and thick. Other jams or jellys inside were hardened too. The cake wasn't soft, but on the harder side. There were passion fruit seeds inside, which means there's passion fruit in one of the layers. So not sure why they call it Yuzu Matcha when there's no yuzu in it.
I don't know what happened, but the pastry selection was sparse that day. When I saw pics on Yelp, it seemed the display case would be filled with a vast array of desserts and pastries. There weren't even any macarons either! None! I was utterly confused and asked the person helping me, "Is this everything?" and he said it was everything. He looked at me as though I was asking the wrong question because one-third of the display case was empty. All in all, a complete disappointment! With so many new places to experience in LA, there's no need to visit again. DON'T WASTE YOUR MONEY HERE!
CUSTOMER SERVICE It was okay. People weren't smiling nor welcoming, just doing the bare minimum.

Christina C. Elite 26 Los Angeles, CA 32 Dessert reviews Jun 30, 2024 I have strictly come only for the pastries, so this review is only for that and not the food service (which I will hopefully try one day).
For the price of their pastries, I would say they are all a great deal. The croissants are HUGE and priced the same as other bakeries around LA which sell smaller and lower quality croissants ($5-6). Take the ham and cheese croissant home, pop in the microwave for 15 seconds and satisfy your savory pastry belly. The apple turnover is HUGE at $5.50. Gonna emphasize that you really get bang for your buck here. On weekends, there's usually a short wait at the pastry counter, but moves pretty fast.
Cakes and tarts are beautifully presented and I've tried almost all of them. They're decent. My opinion on them is that they tend to be on the sweet side. They're a bit pricey, but not exorbitant. If you want to buy one to bring to a house party or whatnot and impress, these are great. But personally, after having tried almost all their cakes, I've moved on to other cake shops.

Luke L. Elite 26 All-Star San Gabriel, CA 39 Italian Food reviews Nov 12, 2023 10 photos I waited for over a decade, but I finally had a chance to visit the #1 most reviewed restaurants and food businesses on Yelp in the United States (https://blog.yelp.com/community/the-top-50-most-reviewed-restaurants-and-eateries-on-yelp/). [BUSINESS NAME] is a restaurant / gourmet market / patisserie and café located in Downtown Los Angeles. The restaurant is one of the most photogenic and popular restaurant in Downtown Los Angeles. [BUSINESS NAME] has another location in West Hollywood.
[Parking]: $20 valet parking and expect to wait 10 minutes to retrieve car. The parking garage across the street (725 S Grand Ave) offers $12 daily max from Monday to Friday before 4 PM ($2.00 every 15 minutes) and $7 in evening after 4 PM. Flat rate $7 on Saturday and Sunday. If you are lucky enough then it is cheaper to park via street metered parking (free metered parking on Sunday). The adjacent Whole Foods parking lot offers free 90 minute parking with validation at Whole Food, but park at your own risk as (1) one time I validated my parking ticket to shop at Whole and Foods and I still ended up paying for my parking ticket and (2) the lot is for Whole Foods customers only.
[Reservation]: The restaurant / cafe eliminated no-reservations policy and customers are now able to book a reservation through Resy. I actually booked a same-day 12:15 PM reservation 75 minutes ahead of the scheduled time.
[Service]: Pretty standard service as expected from an upscale brunch spot like this. Please note that the restaurant automatically adds 5% surcharge to mitigate inflation & other operating costs (it is not a service charge, gratuity, or paid to employees).
[Menu]: Yelp highlighted brunch items include Smoked Salmon Benedict / Eggs Benedict, Portobello Mushroom, Burrata Pizza, and French Toast. Items like Trenne Pasta, Pork Chop, and Braised Prime Rib Eye are only available during dinner hours. Lastly don't forget to take a look at their colorful macarons near the front entrance.
***
Smoked Salmon Benedict (Poached eggs, smoked salmon, crème fraîche, red onions, sautéed spinach & hollandaise sauce on hash brown potato pancakes - $32): This dish was alright and all within expectations - hash brown didn't really enhanced the overall texture for poached eggs and smoked salmon.
Burrata (Mozzarella, burrata, prosciutto di Parma, pecorino romano, sauteed rapini, minced garlic & extra virgin olive oil - 14-inch pizza $36): Amazing cheese and this pizza is definitely a must-order. It was enough portion to share among 3 full sized adults.
This is a perfect restaurant to have a wonderful brunch or dinner with a date; however I am not a fan of 5% "inflation" surcharge as each item on the brunch menu is already pretty expensive. Delicious pizza, decent service, and average benedict. I can't fault anyone for visiting this foodie trap with the spectacular decor and eye-catching baked goods section.

Angelica L. Elite 26 All-Star Austin, TX 54 Brunch reviews Jun 10, 2023Updated review 10 photos Overall: 4.6/5 (dine in)
My friends and I came for coffee and pastries which had a different seating area from the restaurant. They had a great variety of pastries, desserts, and drinks for a reasonable price.
Zeppoli ($5): This is an Italian pastry consisting of a deep-fried dough topped with sugar. It's very flakey, and you get that crunch from the granular sugar. However, it was more on the bland side so I didn't care for it.
Pretzel Cheese ($4.50): This is very different from the pretzels I had at Auntie Anne's or Wetzel's Pretzels. You barely taste the cheese so I'm not sure what I was tasting. I didn't like it but this was my friend's favorite.
Monkey Bread ($5.50): This is an explosion of flavors because it has so many things in there - you get cinnamon, chocolate, & walnut all from one bite. I think it would have been better warm instead of room temperature but it was definitely one of my favorites. A very close first to the chocolate hazelnut supreme croissant.
Chocolate Hazelnut Supreme Croissant ($9): Had to get this since I've been seeing this all over TikTok, and this was my favorite pastry. I love the croissant taste and texture. It had condensed layers so it's not as flakey as the zeppoli but it tasted better. The chocolate hazelnut topping and filling wasn't too sweet but I think there was a bit too much of the chocolate filling. If we didn't split this between the 3 of us, I would say the chocolate would have been overwhelming and the monkey bread would have been my favorite.
16 Oz Iced Lavender Latte With Oat Milk ($6.25): Wow, this was really good. The barista gave me the latte with the layers all separated instead of mixed up. I barely saw a thin line of the lavender syrup so I thought the flavor would be light but I was wrong. The latte was well balanced and delicious!

Elite 26 All-Star Walnut, CA 95 Brunch reviews May 14, 2023Updated review 2 photos This is THE place to be in the morning with a drink and pastry in hand... but I went at night because work obligations call first, unfortunately. Even though I went late in the day, they still had a variety of pastries left of which the raspberry beignet and monkey bread caught my eye.
Monkey bread is such a nostalgic childhood snack for me and this one was a much more eloquent version than the one my mom always made. It had a beautiful flaky outer crust with the most buttery, sweet cinnamon-y center. The pull-apart aspect is my favorite part of the pastry and with every pull, there was a mixture of pecans and chocolate chunks.
The raspberry beignet was like your typical doughnut with a sugary dusting and raspberry filling. There wasn't really a wow factor.
My drink of choice was the Matcha Nitro Latte which I'd never heard of before. The kind lady explained to me that it was ceremonial matcha concentrate with nitrogen-infused cold brew. The result was a really light, airy drink that I honestly could've drank in just 3 sips. We got one regular and one with lavender syrup which I highly recommend. The floral note adds a great depth of flavor to the fluffy matcha drink.
I had such a great experience here overall with both customer service and ambiance. I love natural light and the window seating was perfect. With a large, luxurious pastry selection and a unique drink menu, this is a great place to catch up with friends.

Pranjali S. Elite 26 San Diego, CA 95 Brunch reviews May 14, 2023 10 photos We were lucky to get seated immediately after showing up on a fairly busy Sat during the USC graduation and Mother's Day weekend. I couldn't make reservations online so I assume they didn't have a reservation system.
When you walk in, you get a full view of their bakery section, the delicious macarons and pasties on the right and the open view kitchen on the left. The whole place has a relaxed, modern and fancy-casual feel. It is definitely loud, thanks to the open layout. So you do have to scream into people's ears. The service was very nice but could've been a little more prompt.
We ordered plates to share so we all tasted a bit of everything - the lobster hash, the smoked salmon Benedict, the calamari fritti and the Louis salad. I got an iced matcha green tea latte which was very very good for a matcha lover.
The smoked salmon Benedict was the best of all the food. The salmon had a slight tarty-spicy kick to it which I enjoyed. The hash cake it lay on was also delicious. The hollandaise was well done as was the poached egg. All in all, great recipe.
The lobster hash sounded much better than it was. The hash was delicious. The lobster could've had a little more salt or spice to it as it was pretty bland. The sauce it came in was too liquid and had a yolky taste to it which I didn't particularly love. Some of the greens accompanying the dish were also little bitter for my taste buds. So, a dish that sounded tastier than it was.
The calamari was as expected. Nothing too fancy, nothing too out of the ordinary. It was a nice accompaniment as a smaller plate with 3 other big plates.
The Louis salad was delicious! As a rule, I don't order salads because I don't consider it a full meal. But this salad was a tasty medley of greens, crab, avocadoes and a dressing that got my taste buds singing. It was also a huge portion! Among the four of us, we had a hard time finishing everything.
As a dessert, we first ordered the almond pistachio cherry pastry which deserves an entire star in itself. It looked so so perfect and, if it is even possible, tasted better than it looked! The pistachio jaconde cake was moist and light, the cherry marmalade was perfectly sweet and firm and the almond and pistachio mousse was so so tasty. We cleaned that up!
We then also ordered a limonchello which was a tarty tart with a limonchello curd and limonchello cloud with pralines. Now, as a rule, I'm one of those tart-loving people who complain "not enough lemon, needs more lime..." Well, ladies and gentlemen, not this time! This was so so tarty and just the right level of sweet that I couldn't get enough of it! The tart itself was a little firm and we were so full, I only licked off all the limoncello curd and the cloud. I'm not a fan of pralines, especially when they interfere with my soul-calming experience with limonchello or lemon or lime tartness. So maybe they should get rid of the pralines.
All in all, the food is good but those pastries blew out minds. Come here for the pastries. I wish I could've tried the macarons but we were so full that we could not. So next time it is!

Montebello fries- it's portebello mushroom with a batter and you dip it in the garlic aioli. I am uncertain what the hype is about for the fries. The batter for the fries is pretty thick, almost reminds me of potato wedges. It's good but not that great.
One thing that I didn't like was the fact that they added a 5% surcharge due to inflation. Elite 26 Kissimmee, FL 47 Brunch reviews Feb 20, 2023 2 photos This location has been on a shared bucket list with a good friend of mine, as it is the flagship store and many celebrities have been seen dining here. (Prior to the pandemic, my friend recalled a fan or photographer catching BLACKPINK's Jennie and Red Velvet's Irene enjoying a meal here and playfully fighting over the bill.)
After a fun morning at Melrose Trading Post, my friend and I, plus my dad, came here for brunch. My friend arrived before we did and put our name in. (The wait was 5 minutes max before we were seated.) Please note if you come before 3 PM on the weekends, the brunch menu is served through to 3 PM and they cannot accommodate their regular menu. (They have some of their staples like a few pasta dishes and pizzas, but you won't find the full offering on the brunch menu.)
There are two choices for parking - either you can do valet for the restaurant which is a flat fee of $30 or you can find a lot or garage near the restaurant and park and walk. Both my friend and I did the latter and found a structure across the street that charged $7 flat fee for the day. The walk to the restaurant was very short - go up to one of the crosswalks and cross, then go up to the restaurant on the corner.
Staff were polite and very on top of service - they would notice empty plates or when you were finished with something and would clear it for space on your table.
My friend ordered the carbonara, I ordered a pizza, and my dad got an eggs Benedict. We shared the beignets at the table.
The beignets were dusted in sugar and came with a tart raspberry sauce to dip/drizzle on top. The flavors balanced out nicely and kept the beignet from skewing too sweet or heavy.
Food came out a couple of minutes after we finished the beignets. They make the pizzas fresh in one of the hot ovens and we were sitting near one of the areas where they were making them. (It was fun watching our food from start to finish.)
Their sauce is good and they were generous with the toppings. The crust has a good crunch but I will admit with the sauce and thin crust that it could have the potential to become soggy at times if you didn't eat it right away.
My friend enjoyed her carbonara and my dad liked the Benedict.
I will admit this location is very different aesthetically from the one in West Hollywood. This one has more of an upscale, classic architecture with visible kitchen spaces and often it becomes quite noisy in there with everyone talking at once. Tables are put together a bit closer than the West Hollywood one so I would say this one feels more close in terms of how they packed parties in and maybe at times a little crowded. In contrast, West Hollywood was more relaxed but still had a balance of elegance and contemporary elements in the space. (Also it can be a bit of a tourist destination for people who hear about the restaurant or saw their favorite celebrities dine here once.)
I noticed the flagship didn't have as many flavors of their macarons out as they did at West Hollywood. It seems like this one was focusing more on the cakes and pastries.
Prices are mid to high - I would say this is a fun experience to do once in a while, but unless you have the means to do it more often, it can be a little pricey.

Though it is pretty expensive for brunch, the quality and taste of the food was totally worth it. Out of everything we tried, the french toast, avocado toast, and OJ is a must-try. Everything is pretty basic, but each dish was so well-executed that you NEED to come here at least once. We came for Saturday brunch but we were able to be seated right away. The restaurant itself is gorgeous and it's a great view/ambiance to take in while dining. The service was amazing as well; our waiter was beyond attentive and friendly which really enhanced our whole experience. Be sure to also grab some pastries on the way out; the worker who was helping us even snuck in an extra earl gray macaron in our box!
Though we visited from NYC, we will be sure to come back whenever we are in the area.

Do not hesitate to visit this spot.

The money they must make here is absolutely insane, so I pray they use it to pay their workers vs upping our fees. But that may be a different conversation for a different day.
All around: 8.5/10 The parking is hell on earth, the prices are a bit high, and the dessert does not miss. . I can cannot lie, I just might be back sooner than I think! If you can get past the traffic, parking, and prices, you'll just love it here too!!! :)

Sammy B. Elite 26 Whittier, CA 22 Dessert reviews Sep 16, 2023 10 photos [BUSINESS NAME] feels like how it feels to walk into a Hermes or Louis Vuitton: bougie. The air is filled with energy matched with this atmosphere of excitement for the food and undeniable amounts of photos ready to be taken. The interior design is clean white with elements of gold and wood to accent such pristine decor. If they were hanging clothes next to each table and fashionable bags on the walls you would think you were dining in a Hermes or Louis Vuitton. Large windows bring in extra light along with the gaze of both the envious and curious walking by. The interior could be placed in any modern home spread or fashion magazine and it would make sense it made it there.
The service is on point. Once you get seated the waiters will come by to ask for your drinks along with giving insight to the specials and favorites of the restaurant. If you haven't done research yet you can always look around as both the Hollywood elite and the common man will have faces of excitement when the food arrives. [BUSINESS NAME] is also known for their brunches so even better if you arrived at such hours.
You cannot gain notoriety in the food world, much less Los Angeles, unless your food makes a statement and that's what [BUSINESS NAME] does: consistently delicious food done simply but with an air of classiness. But what does that actually mean? Let's dissect some food to figure that out.
Let's look at an appetizer that feels familiar but spins it in a different direction. At [BUSINESS NAME] they've created a masterpiece of food utilizing the umami flavors of a mushroom along with the familiarity of fries. The Portobello fries are large cut slices of portobello mushrooms in the shape of giant wedges. They are fried golden and topped with parsley and parmesan cheese. They come with an aioli to dip with and these things are phenomenal. Though fried the mushrooms aren't dripping in oil. Though a mushroom these won't be soggy to the bite. With the sharpness of the cheese, the creaminess of the aioli, and the earthy, almost meaty flavor of the mushroom this appetizer is highly elevated in all regards.
How about something sweet? [BUSINESS NAME] knows all about sweets but incorporates balance. The French Toast here is a must try. Using a pain de mie bread (common type of white or brown bread used in France with a soft, thin crust) these thick cut slices contain so many different flavors all telling a cohesive story. You have a slight savory flavor from the egg base, a citrus component from the fresh berries and compote, richness from the buttered sliced almonds, and the blast of sweetness from maple syrup to drizzle on the side. All different notes creating a symphony to this breakfast aria.
What of savory dishes? So many to choose from. You can have their Croque Madame, which for those who never have had one before, is like an elevated ham sandwich with a poached/over easy egg on top and béchamel sauce. The Calamari Fritti are lightly fried bouncy pieces of circular squid, a delight for both flavors and mouth feels for those who enjoy texture. And of course, the pizzas. They are phenomenal. All pizzas are cooked over an open fire in a wooden oven and you get that nice smokey flavor along with the char at the edges. They have a variety of different types ranging from the classics such as the Margherita pizza to something with more kick like the Calabrese pizza which has Calabrese salami, fennel confit, pickled Fresno chiles, fresh mozzarella, Pecorino Romano, and Malibu honey. At times they will also have speciality pizzas, for example I had one with their homemade sausage, shaved truffles, and over easy egg. It was so indulgent. Pro-tip: if you do get the pizzas let them rest when they arrive for at least five minutes. Otherwise, you'll have scorching hot breath from these fiery pizzas.
Lastly, you cannot forget the patisserie at the front of the restaurant. No reservations needed to check these well crafted pastries. Not only will each one be a visual delight but the flavors match swimmingly. They are most known for their array of different macrons and when you see them in person you'll understand why. When you buy them they have well designed boxes to wrap them in, making them a fantastic gift to someone or yourself. Rave over their glossy finishes for their cakes and the bountiful layers in their croissants.
[BUSINESS NAME] is an experience all in itself. Elevated dining is an understatement to this experience. Wrapped with the luxury of its atmosphere to the different levels of flavors it's no surprise why people from all backgrounds enjoy this restaurant. If you want to as well, make sure you bring your appetite, your camera, and a fat wallet.
